A caller without document units refuses a length suffix rather than assuming millimetres. Typed parameters (Stage 1a, ZC-093/094/096, this PR): schema v21 adds an optional `quantity` (length, angle or number) to each parameter. A typed parameter carries its dimension into every expression that reads it, so `width + 5 mm` and `width + 1 in` evaluate while a length in an angle field, a typed number with units and `width + 5` (a bare number added to a length) are refused on the existing throw path; absent stays the unitless parameter every older document holds, so no document is rewritten or retyped on load. A quantity change is refused, naming the use, when any stored expression that reads the parameter would no longer hold its field's dimension or stay coherent (every expression field is now tagged length/angle/number), and parameters reading it are re-checked the same way. Each unitless parameter is offered the quantity its own units or all of its uses agree on, in the Build sidebar's type picker, and applied only when the owner picks it. A reference cycle is reported as one (`a → b → a`) instead of an unknown identifier. Regression evidence: `packages/document-core/src/typed-parameters.test.ts` (mixed units, untyped behaviour unchanged, field refusals, quantity refusals by field/bare number/dependent parameter, named cycles, suggestions), `test/typed-parameters.test.ts` (exact build of a `width + 5 mm` box, an inch edit, a refused clear and undo), `ParameterRows.test.tsx` and `typed-parameters.spec.ts`. Open: document-unit threading for the remaining modeling helpers/previews; quantity in the AI parameter operations; replay still rounds a count expression that later evaluates to a fraction (ZC-095 advisory). The sketch/extrusion F05/U01 slice now supplies document units to entity fields, History sketch/text editors, text creation and placement, and extrusion summaries, semantic validity checks, active sketch/region rendering, form previews, exact whole-object profiles, extrusion side lineage and two-sided spans; length, angle and dimensionless count fields use the existing typed evaluator and keep authored expressions. Regression evidence: `SketchEntityEditor.test.tsx`, `ExtrudeForm.test.tsx`, `test/sketch-extrude-unit-expressions.test.ts`, `test/parity/sketch-extrude-unit-context.spec.ts` and `test/e2e/sketch-extrude-unit-context.spec.ts` cover equivalent mm/cm/inch geometry, dimensional refusals, preview/commit, Enter/Escape, undo/redo and reopen. This is a caller-integration slice; grammar, parameter schema and document-wide units are unchanged. Delivered 2026-10-11 (G5 in `docs/reviews/design-review-2026-10-11.md`, PR #626; this PR #635): a parameter can be defined from the expression field that first names it. Every `ExprInput` replaces "Unknown identifier" with "`width` is not defined yet · Define width = [last value]" when the only problem is undefined parameter names, decided by the evaluator's own parse with the missing names recorded as it reads them, never by sample values (`undefinedParameterIn` in `apps/web/src/lib/model.ts`, so `sqrt(width - 1000)` still offers `width`); syntax, trailing-token, reserved-name and unit/dimension refusals keep their text. The definition runs the Parameters panel's validated set as its own undo step before the feature, never overwrites an existing name, and Inspector edit drafts survive it. Evidence: `apps/web/src/lib/model.test.ts`, `ExprInput.test.tsx`, `PrimitiveForm.test.tsx`, `fieldParameterDefinitions.test.ts`, `test/e2e/define-parameter-from-field.spec.ts`. Still open: parameter unit types; a definition and its feature land as two undo steps by decision. 2026-10-11 queue integration: Integrated review #635 and #638: define parameters from expression fields as a separate undo step, parse the full expression before offering a definition, atomically refuse names added during an awaited patch, and resolve primitive unit suffixes in document units in both Remus and the OCCT parity reference. Coverage: model, ExprInput, inline-parameter-definition-race, primitive-unit-expressions and imperial-unit-expressions. 14 October review follow-up TEST-3 (this PR): 121 independently authored field cases across 32 feature, direct-edit and sketch variants now prove parameter reference discovery, deletion refusal, exact rename, source/literal preservation and serialized command replay (`test/parameter-expression-fields.test.ts`). A disposable mutation removing fourteen visitor field keys leaves the existing rename/delete suites green but fails seventeen new cases, reproducing and closing the coverage gap from the [project review](docs/reviews/project-review-2026-10-14.md). Datum planes and axes are covered per definition kind (canonical source offsets and point coordinates), so deleting any datum visitor call fails its cases too. Runtime behavior is unchanged. Pattern count advisory (ZC-095, this PR): entry already refuses a fractional count, and a count expression that later evaluates to one (`seats / 2` after `seats` changes) still rounds as saved documents rely on, but now raises a typed advisory on the pattern naming the value and the rounded count; regression evidence `pattern-count-advisory.test.ts`. 13 November design review, Build entry (I25, I33 and the parameter parts of I11): a name no parameter defines yet is offered for definition in place from the numeric keypad and from a command's Options rows too, not only from an expression field ("foo is not defined yet · Define foo = [30]"); defined, the keypad previews the entry and an Options row applies it. While either entry does not evaluate, Done waits instead of applying the value held, and an Options row says why it was not applied. The keypad readout names the unit once ("= 30" beside its unit, no longer "= 30 mm mm"). A new parameter whose expression states a unit takes that quantity (`width = 40 mm` is a length, no longer a unitless 40); the status lane says a refused new name "was not added" rather than "was not changed", reports a taken name the add row refuses, and the expression cell grows to show `width / 2` where the field can size to its content. Coverage: `NumericKeypad.test.tsx`, `InPlaceCommandBar.test.tsx`, `ParameterRows.focus.test.tsx`, `parameterQuantity.test.ts`. | Existing parameter/expression tests; U01 for field behaviour; [comparison §2.8](docs/reviews/reference-cad-comparison-2026-09-26.md). Units stay document-wide (I03 unchanged). Research-audit Stage 0: pattern counts are refused at entry unless whole (`FeatureForms.validation.test.tsx`); replay still rounds a count expression that later evaluates to a fraction. |
| <a id="f06"></a>F06 | Complete | Configurations: a variant table on the document whose rows are named configurations and whose columns are parameters, feature suppression, body visibility and material; one active configuration per view and per assembly instance; exact export of any row; and a Tweak link that selects a row and exports STL or 3MF, so a parametric part can be shared as a customizer. Grows from the L05 on/off body parameters; switching rows must rebuild within W01 budgets. Exact export of any row ([design](docs/plans/configurations-design.md)): each configuration row has an Export picker (STEP, or the mesh dialog for 3MF, STL and the rest), available without edit access, and the mesh dialog names the configuration it writes with a picker to change it. A row that is not the one shown is built for the file alone from `documentForConfiguration`; nothing in the open document changes, and the file holds every body that row shows (the kernel adapter's new `documentExportBodyIds`, sharing `exportStepBodies`' choice of shown, unconsumed, unsuppressed bodies), so a row that suppresses a feature or shows a body the displayed row hides exports correctly. The printability check and the slicer hand-off use the same row, and the file is named after it. Evidence: `test/configuration-export.test.ts` (each row's body list, the hidden row's STEP re-imported as a 45 mm plate and tag without the lug, the displayed row unchanged, 3MF of the hidden row), `ExportDialog.test.tsx` and `ConfigurationTable.test.tsx` (the pickers), and `test/e2e/configuration-export.spec.ts` (STEP and 3MF of a row that is not shown, re-imported and measured, the model left as it was, and the dialog switched back to the shown row). One configuration per assembly instance and per model view (schema v90, built on v89; F06 first claimed v79 and then v86, which v80, v81 and v89 overtook; [design](docs/plans/configurations-design.md)): an assembly component may name the configuration it shows (`configurationId`, set from the part's details in Assemble and undone like any assembly edit), and the View workspace has its own Configuration picker above its parts. Each named row is built on its own from `documentForConfiguration` and drawn in place of the active row (a part keeps its placement; a body the part's row suppresses or hides is not drawn), while the document's active row stays the model being edited; rows rebuild only when the geometry changes, not when a part moves. A component cannot name a row the table does not hold, a row a component shows cannot be deleted, and the choice is validated on load and gated to v90. Per-drawing-view configurations follow after the D03 drawing work lands. Evidence: `test/configuration-instances.test.ts` (set, undo, clear and refuse a component's row; deletion refused; storage validation and gate; which rows build; building a row without touching the document), `AssemblyPanel.test.tsx` and `ViewModeRail.test.tsx` (the pickers), and `test/e2e/configuration-instances.spec.ts` (a part drawn 45 mm wide as Large while the model stays Small, through undo, redo and reload; View showing Large without the lug, and Build still editing Small). Row switches within the W01 budgets ([record](docs/performance/2026-11-17-f06-row-switch.md)): Switch → frame for a configuration row, measured beside W01's Apply → frame in the same session on W01's fixtures, each switch to a width no build had used. Three quiet-host rounds of 30 on the W01 host: a simple box switches in 42 ms median, 48 ms p95 (budget 60/100); the 100-feature history with its isolated part in 87 ms median, 116 ms p95 (budget 150/170). Evidence: `test/e2e/perf-configuration-switch.spec.ts` (the probe, `OZ_PERF=1`), the `f06-switch-box` and `f06-switch-history` probes of `scripts/measure-active1-baselines.mjs --check`, and `test/perf-host.test.ts` (the quiet-host check no longer counts jobs queued behind the heavy-job lock; `--heavy-lock` takes that lock per round). Tweak link that selects a row ([design](docs/plans/configurations-design.md)): a share link can name a configuration after its capability (`#share=<token>&configuration=<row id>`); only the row id travels, and its values come from the shared model, so a link can pick only a row its author defined. The shared model opens on that row (a row the model no longer has, or cannot take, opens it as saved and says so). Tweak gains a Configuration picker above the parameters, and switching rows is Tweak vocabulary like a value edit: allowed in a shared session and undone by Tweak's undo, while editing the table itself stays a Build edit. The sharing dialog's new link has an "Opens on" choice that rewrites the link before it is copied. Evidence: `test/project-share-link.test.ts` (reading, writing and replacing the row without disturbing plaintext or encrypted capabilities), `test/configurations.test.ts` (row switch is a Tweak command and Tweak-undoable, table edits are not; opening a shared model on a named, missing or unbuildable row), `TweakPanel.test.tsx` and `ProjectSharingDialog.test.tsx` (the picker and the Opens on choice), and `test/e2e/configuration-tweak-link.spec.ts` (a link naming Large opens on it, exports 3MF re-imported at 1808 mm³, switches to Small in Tweak and exports STL re-imported at 864 mm³, and undo returns to Large). Variant table and active row (schema v72, built on v70 with v71 claimed by M17; [design](docs/plans/configurations-design.md)): Parameters gains a Configurations table whose rows are named configurations and whose columns are parameters, feature suppression, body visibility and material. The table is dense and exactly one row is active. The active row's parameter and suppression values are the model's own, so rebuilds, panels and exports read it unchanged; switching rows copies the live values back into the row being left and writes the new row into the model as one undoable step, and a parameter or suppression cell of the active row edits the model directly. Bodies the active row hides are hidden and left out of STEP and mesh exports where an L05 off switch hides them (the exact adapter's export filter included), and the Inspector's mass properties use the active row's material. A row the model cannot take is refused naming the row; the section, its history and its commands are validated on load and gated to v72; diagnostic reproductions replace row names. Evidence: `test/configurations.test.ts` (dense table, activation round trip keeping edits in the row left, hidden bodies, refusals, rename/delete, export document for another row, storage gate and validation, undo/redo/replay and history validation, diagnostic redaction, exact rebuild of each row with export filtering), `ConfigurationTable.test.tsx` and `test/e2e/configurations.spec.ts` (build a table, switch, undo/redo, reload). One configuration per drawing view (schema v89, built on v81 with v82, v84, v85, v87 and v88 claimed by D02/D03, v83 by M17 and v86 by F06 slice 4a; [design](docs/plans/configurations-design.md)): a base drawing view may name the configuration row it draws (`configurationId`, set from the view's settings in Draw and undone like any drawing edit); every view folded, cut, detailed or seen square from it draws the same row, and says so. The view's bodies are tessellated and sectioned from `documentForConfiguration` while the document's active row stays the model being edited; tessellations, section planes and projections are keyed by the row and the model content, so a row view never shares geometry with the active row's and is drawn again when that row can have changed, while views on the active row keep exactly their keys. A derived view cannot name its own row, a view cannot name a row the table does not hold, a row (or the whole table) a view shows cannot be deleted, and the choice is validated on load and in history and gated to v89. Evidence: `test/configuration-views.test.ts` (set, undo, clear and refuse a view's row; deletion refused; validation and the v89 gate; derived views drawing their source's row; projection keys apart from the active row and following the model content) and `test/e2e/configuration-views.spec.ts` (a front view switched to Large draws 45 mm wide at 1:1 and its projected top view follows, while Build still edits the 20 mm Small; back on the active row both draw 20 mm). All F06 slices are on main: variant table and active row (#1645), exact export of any row (#1647), a Tweak link that opens on a row and exports STL/3MF (#1661), one configuration per drawing view (#1677), one configuration per assembly part and per View-mode view (#1672), and row switches measured within the W01 budgets (#1680; simple box 42/48 ms against 60/100 ms, 100-feature history 87/116 ms against 150/170 ms, median/p95). A row switch, an active-row cell, an assistant `set_parameter` patch and every read-only row build (a view or component on a row, a Tweak link's row, a row export) solve again the sketches whose dimensions, offsets, angles or pattern spacing the new values drive, storing the solved geometry in the same history entry (`apps/web/src/lib/sketch/parameterResolve.ts`; `test/parameter-change-sketch-resolve.test.ts`, `test/e2e/configuration-sketch-resolve.spec.ts`). | F05 expressions; L05 body parameters; AS01 for per-instance choice; H02/W01 rebuild cost. Split from L05 on 2026-11-15. |

## 2. Part modeling, inspection and exchange

| ID | Status | Outcome and completion evidence | Depends on / legacy item |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| <a id="m01"></a>M01 | Partial | Multi-position/sketch-point holes, standards tables and cosmetic-thread/callout metadata, then hole handles; test parameter combinations and history edits. Slice 4, cosmetic threads and callout metadata for D02 (schema v67, up from v66; no kernel change). `tapped.cosmetic: true` describes a tapped hole's thread without cutting it: the bore stays its tap drill and keeps its `hole.face.bore` name, and a cosmetic thread reads no print clearance and raises no print advice. Callout metadata: `HoleCallout` (shared) is what any reader of a hole gets: the count of bores, the bore's diameter (the table's for a standard), its depth or through, the style, the standard, and the thread (`M6×1` or `1/4-20 UNC`, major diameter and pitch in document units, length or the whole bore, hand, cosmetic or cut). document-core's `holeCallout(document, featureId)` reads it from the stored hole as the build does, and `holeOfBoreFace` names the hole a face is a bore of from its lineage: directly, on sketch points (`hole.face.at.<point>.bore`) or as an M02 pattern copy (`pattern.face.instance.<n>.source.<hole>.…`). `holeCalloutLabel` writes it as a drawing does, numbers in the drawing's precision: `M6×1 THRU`, `3× M6×1 LH × 8 DEEP, Ø5 × 12 DEEP`, `Ø6.6 THRU, M6 CLEARANCE`, `Ø5 × 12 DEEP, M6×1 TAP DRILL`. D02 reads it: a diameter dimension on the rim of a hole's bore, whose hole has a standard or a thread and whose rim is the bore's own diameter, is labelled with the callout instead of the bare diameter, on screen and in PDF/SVG export (`resolveSheetAnnotations` takes the drawing workspace's lookup, which a sheet kept from before a model change keeps from the document it was drawn from until its Update). A content gate refuses `cosmetic` in a document that declares v66 or older. UI: a tapped hole's Options gain Thread form (Modeled, Cosmetic); a cosmetic thread keeps its draggable length and drops the fit. History reads "M6×1 tapped (cosmetic) × 10 mm". Evidence: `test/hole-callouts.test.ts` (15 cases: the callout text for plain, clearance, tap-drill, through, whole-blind, stopped-short left-hand three-point and UNC holes; bore-face naming directly, on points and as a pattern copy; `holeCallout` reading the table's diameter over a stored one, the thread and its form and the point count; the v67 gate and the print-clearance read; on the pinned kernel a cosmetic M6 hole whose volume is the plain tap drill's, closed form, with no advice, and a D02 diameter on its rim in the top view reading `M6×1 THRU`, `M6×1 × 6 DEEP, Ø5 × 8 DEEP` and `Ø5 THRU, M6×1 TAP DRILL`, and `Ø5` without the lookup; a feature pattern leaving a cosmetic thread's copies plain tap drills; a kept sheet's lookup still reading the drawn depth after the model deepens the hole); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts` and `edit.test.ts`; `test/e2e/hole-callouts.spec.ts`, which clicks the block's top face, chooses a tapped M6 with a cosmetic thread, drags the thread's arrow short of the blind bore, then in Draw picks the bore's rim in the top view for a diameter and finds it called out as `M6×1 × <length> DEEP, Ø5 × 10 DEEP`. Open: drawing the cosmetic thread itself (the thin major-diameter circle in an end view, its lines in a side view) and hole tables on drawing sheets. Slice 1, multi-position holes on sketch points (schema v64, up from v63; no kernel change, Remus 2026.1.141). A `hole` may store `points`: an earlier sketch and the I01 point entities it is drilled at. Each point is read on its sketch plane, which must be parallel to the entry face, and dropped onto the face along its normal, so a sketch on the face or on an offset plane places its bores the same way; `position` is then stored but not read. Every bore takes the hole's size, style and depth (a through bore clears the body from its own entry point), is cut and proven one at a time as a single hole is, and is named by its point (`hole.face.at.<point id>.bore` and `.counterbore`; shoulders and floors that share a plane stay unnamed, as a pattern's copies do). A moved point carries its bore. Refused by name: a missing sketch, a later sketch, an entity that is not a point, an empty or repeated list, a deleted point, a sketch that is not parallel to the face, and a point whose bore misses the body (`At sketch point <id>: …`). Clearing `points` returns the hole to its one stored position. An M02 feature pattern repeats such a hole whole, each copy's bores named by copy and point. History makes the sketch a parent of the hole, and an edit to the sketch revalidates it. A content gate refuses holes on sketch points in a document that declares v63 or older. UI (U11 pick-and-drag): the Hole tool's Position gains Sketch points. After the face, clicks on a sketch's points (drawn as small crossed marks) choose where bores go; the diameter handle stands on the first point and sizes every bore. A History edit reopens with the points as picks and writes them back unchanged; switching back to Clicked or Typed clears them. History reads "⌀ 6 mm through at 3 points". Evidence: `test/hole-points.test.ts` (17 cases on the pinned kernel: through bores at four points with closed-form volume, axes and per-point names; blind counterbores at two points; points dropped from a parallel sketch on the plate's bottom; a moved point carried and a deleted point refused; a point off the face refused by its id; a non-parallel sketch refused; switching between one position and points; an M02 linear feature pattern repeating a two-point hole to six bores; the document, ordering and v64-gate refusals); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts`, `edit.test.ts`, `featureHistory.test.ts`, `affectedFeatureTargets.test.ts`; `test/e2e/hole-points.spec.ts`, which picks the plate's top face, sets Position to Sketch points, clicks three sketch points, drags the diameter handle and checks the stored hole and its History row. Budget: the feature-pattern hole path changed, so the 10 × 10 grid preview was re-measured (`node scripts/measure-active1-baselines.mjs --only pattern-node,pattern-browser`, 3 runs each): 7,565 ms in the browser and 7,630 ms in Node, against the 10,000 ms gate (M02 slice 2: 7,571 and 7,645 ms). Open: ISO/ANSI size tables, tapped holes on M15 thread data, cosmetic-thread and callout metadata for D02, and hole handles. Slice 2, ISO and ANSI size tables (schema v65, up from v64; no kernel change). A `hole` may store `standard`: `{ system: 'iso' \| 'ansi', size, kind: 'clearance' \| 'tap-drill', fit? }`. Its table gives the bore's diameter, converted to the document's units; every add and edit stores that value as `diameter` (whatever the writer sent), and the build reads the table. Tables (`packages/shared/src/hole-standards.ts`), each in its standard's own units: ISO 273 clearance holes in the fine, medium and coarse series as close, normal and loose fits, and ISO 2306 tap drills, for every ISO 261 coarse size M1.6–M36; for UNC #0–1 in, the close and free (normal) clearance drills of the common machinist's table (it has no loose fit) and the 75% tap drills. Refused by name: an unknown system or size, a fit on a tap drill, and an ANSI loose fit. Clearing `standard` keeps the bore it was drilled to. Counterbore and countersink suggestions are worked out in millimetres, so a metre document's small screw keeps them. It composes with slice 1: every bore on sketch points takes the table's size. A content gate refuses a standard in a document that declares v64 or older. UI: the Hole tool's Options gain Standard (Any, ISO metric, ANSI inch), Screw, For (Clearance, Tap drill) and Fit. A standard removes the diameter handle, since the table sets it, and the hint reads it out ("M8×1.25 tap drill ⌀ 6.8"); counterbore and countersink defaults follow the table's bore. A History edit reopens with the standard chosen, and Any clears it. History reads "M6 clearance (normal) through". Evidence: `test/hole-standards.test.ts` (22 cases: every tap drill falls between its thread's basic minor diameter `D − 1.0825 P` and `D − P/2`; every clearance hole clears its screw with each fit wider than the last; the sizes match the M15 ISO 261 table; spot values as printed; labels; refusals; the v65 gate; on the pinned kernel an M6 clearance hole drilled to Ø6.6 whatever the stored diameter, a blind M8 tap drill to Ø6.8 by closed-form volume, keeping it when cleared; the table diameter stored on add, on edit and in an inch document, and a stale stored diameter ignored by the build, a 1/4-20 tap drill in an inch document to 0.201 in, and two M4 tap drills on sketch points); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts` and `edit.test.ts`; `test/e2e/hole-standards.spec.ts`, which clicks the block's top face, chooses ISO metric, M8 and Tap drill in Options, checks there is no diameter handle, drags the blind depth arrow and checks the stored hole and its History row. Slice 3, tapped holes on M15 thread data (schema v66, up from v65; no kernel change, Remus 2026.1.141). A hole sized as a standard tap drill may store `tapped: { length?, clearance?, hand? }`. After the bores are drilled, each one is threaded by the M15 thread generator (`cutThreadDetailed`, through the `cutResolvedThread` helper the Thread feature now shares). The thread is the standard's size and pitch with the ISO 68-1 internal form, which is also the Unified basic profile, so UNC sizes need no form of their own. It starts at the bore's end nearer the entry face and runs the whole bore unless `length` stops it short, and the tap-drill bore truncates its crest as a Thread's. A hole that sets no clearance reads the L09 document print clearance, exactly as a Thread with the Document fit does: `featureReadsPrintClearance` counts it, so the rebuild cache digests the resolved clearance for it too. Print advice is raised once per hole. An M02 feature pattern that repeats a tapped hole taps every copy the same way, each copy's bore proven one plain cylinder before it is threaded. Refused by name: tapping a hole that is not a standard tap drill, a hand other than right or left, a non-positive length or a negative clearance, and, through the thread generator's own codes, a thread longer than its bore (per point, `At sketch point <id>: …`, on sketch points). A content gate refuses `tapped` in a document that declares v65 or older. UI: with For set to Tap drill, the Hole tool's Options offer Thread (Drill only, Tapped) and Thread fit (Document, Exact). A tapped hole wears a Thread arrow beside the bore, standing at the depth until it is dragged short, which then stores the length. A History edit reopens with the thread as stored, its hand included, and Drill only clears it. History reads "M6×1 tapped × 10 mm". Evidence: `test/tapped-holes.test.ts` (12 cases; each build is checked against an M15 Thread cut into the same untapped tap-drill bore, to the volume: an M6 through hole; a blind M6 thread stopped 5 mm from the entry, its thread faces all within those 5 mm and the plain bore below; the document print clearance of 0.1 read as a Document-fit Thread reads it, and an Exact fit ignoring it; 1/4-20 UNC in an inch document; two tapped bores on sketch points taking twice one's material; a linear feature pattern of a tapped hole taking three threaded holes' material, not one threaded and two plain; a thread longer than its bore refused by the generator; the contract, print-clearance read and v66 gate); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts` and `edit.test.ts`; `test/e2e/tapped-holes.spec.ts`, which clicks the block's top face, chooses ISO metric, M6, Tap drill and Tapped, drags the Thread arrow short of the blind bore and checks the stored hole and its History row. Cosmetic threads drawn in drawing views (no schema change). A cosmetic thread (slice 4) keeps the tap-drill bore in the model, so no projected edge shows it; each drawing view now draws it by the ISO 6410-1 convention (`apps/web/src/lib/drawing/cosmeticThreads.ts`). Every bore of a view's bodies that a hole with a cosmetic thread drilled is found by its lineage: the hole's own bore, a bore at a sketch point, or an M02 pattern copy's. Seen down the bore, the view draws the major diameter as a thin three-quarter circle, open quarter upper right, where the bore's nearer end opens toward the viewer with nothing of the view's bodies in front of it (a ray against their display meshes); it is hidden otherwise. Seen across the bore, the view draws the major diameter as two hidden lines along the thread's length and a hidden limit line across its end. Which end of a bore is its entry is read from the model as it stands: a blind bore opens at the end its floor (a drill point or a flat disc) does not close; a thread stopped short in a through bore starts at the end on the hole's entry face, found now by the name its stored reference gives (`HoleCallout.entryFace`, optional); a bore where that cannot be settled draws no thread rather than a guessed one. Views that see the bore obliquely draw none, and neither do section views and views of another configuration row. A view drawn without hidden lines draws no hidden thread line. The threads are drawn on screen and in PDF, SVG and DXF exports through `composeSheetPage`, clipped to a detail view's circle, from the model a kept sheet was drawn from until its Update. Evidence: `test/cosmetic-thread-drawing.test.ts` (on the pinned kernel, M6 tap drills in a 10 mm plate: a 6 mm thread in an 8 mm blind bore read from its open end; the Ø6 three-quarter circle from 90° round to 360°, thin from both sides of a through bore and hidden under a blind one's floor; a 4 mm thread in a through bore starting on the entry face; the arc hidden under a lifted cover body and seen without it; two hidden lines ±3 off the axis running 6 down and a 6 wide limit line in the front view, none without hidden lines; nothing in the isometric, a section or another configuration row; three threads for a three-copy feature pattern and none for a plain tap drill; the arc on a composed 1:1 sheet at the view's centre). Hole tables on drawing sheets (schema v117, up from v116). A `hole-table` drawing annotation names one view and its place; everything it prints is read from the model each time the sheet is drawn. Every bore the view sees down its axis is a row: a hole's own bore, a bore at a sketch point, or an M02 pattern copy's, found by its face's lineage (`holeOfBoreFace`). Each row has a tag, X and Y, and a size. Tags take a letter per size, smallest first, and a number per bore within it, left to right, then bottom to top. X and Y are measured in the view from the lower-left corner of its bodies' bounding boxes, in the sheet's number format. The size is the hole's callout written once per bore (`Ø5 THRU, M6×1 TAP DRILL`). The table is ruled as a parts list is, and the view carries each tag up and right of its bore's rim. A view that sees no bore down its axis leaves the table broken, with a reason that says to place it on a view looking into the holes. Refused: a table with no view, one stating a value of its own, and below v117 by the content gate. UI: Add hole table in Sheet Properties (or Y; the phone-width annotation rail has no row to spare, as for general tolerances). One click takes the view, the next places the table's top-left corner, which then moves with its view. The table is drawn on screen and in PDF, SVG and DXF exports. Evidence: `test/hole-tables.test.ts` (the stored shape and its refusals; the v117 gate; the tool's two clicks; on the pinned kernel a 60 × 40 plate with a Ø8 hole and an M6 tap drill repeated by a linear pattern, read in the top view as A1 40/10 and A2 50/10 `Ø5 THRU, M6×1 TAP DRILL`, B1 15/20 `Ø8 THRU`, the B1 tag beside its bore; broken in the front view); `test/e2e/hole-table.spec.ts`, which drills a hole in place, chooses Add hole table in Sheet Properties, clicks the top view and then the paper, and finds the A1 row at 20/15 with its tag in the view and the stored table on that view. Editing the last of 32 simple sketch-point bores on rectangular stock reuses 31 checked cuts. The exact adapter median fell from 155.09 ms on the historical build and 159.99 ms on the parent to 60.45 ms. Geometry, bore names and refusal coverage are recorded in [the multi-position Hole benchmark](docs/performance/2026-11-20-sketch-point-hole-cuts.md). Hole handles audited against U01/U11 (no schema change). Audited: the viewport handles (diameter rim, depth and thread arrows, the counterbore/countersink and position chips, drag preview and release), the chip keypad, the in-place card and its Options, the History re-arm edit, display-unit read-outs and typed entry, and cancel, Escape, undo/redo and refusals. Fixed: (1) a clicked hole's handles stood on the raw click while the preview and commit drilled it at the click rounded to the sketch snap, so the rim and arrows sat up to half a snap step off the bore; they now stand where the request drills it, from the same function. (2) Switching a clicked hole's Position to Typed dropped it to (0, 0); U and V now carry the position it was drilled at (Typed chosen again keeps what was typed). (3) A standard hole's hint read its table diameter in the stored unit, unlabelled, beside Options and chips in the display unit; it now reads in the display unit, with its symbol, when they differ. (4) A tapped hole stored with a clearance of its own reopened with no Thread fit chosen; it reopens on an "As saved" choice and writes the clearance back unchanged. (5) A refused Hole request took its preview down and left the handles on the refused value; like Fillet and Chamfer it now keeps the last frame that built, its handles and chips standing on the values that frame built from, and names the input that changed ("Counterbore ⌀: …"), not the steps that only followed a new bore. (6) Picking a bore, or its rim, of a hole on sketch points (`hole.face.at.<point>.bore`) named no feature, so Edit, Delete and the read-out missed it; it names its Hole. (7) A standard hole's chips were spaced from the stale typed diameter, not the table's bore. Verified unchanged: one value owner across chip, keypad and Options (each writes the same session value the request reads); an untouched edit writes the hole back as stored; Escape and Cancel commit nothing; a thread arrow dragged past its bore is refused by the kernel by name. Evidence: `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts` (snapped handles, Typed carry, display-unit hint, standard spacing, refused inputs), `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/edit.test.ts` ("As saved" fit), `test/hole-points.test.ts` (on the pinned kernel each point's bore and rim resolve to the hole), and `test/e2e/hole-handles-contract.spec.ts`: a click at (23.4, 16.6) puts the diameter ball on the rim of the bore at the snapped (23, 17) within 1.5 px; the dragged size is the chip's text and the stored diameter; Typed reads U −2, V 3 and stores them; a keypad 8 and an Options blind depth 7 are what the History edit stores; Escape after a typed size and a drag leaves the stored hole deep-equal and History unchanged; Undo and Redo restore each committed hole exactly; a too-narrow Ø4 counterbore is named "Counterbore ⌀", its chip stays on the kept Ø10.8 rim within 2 px, and Escape leaves no hole; in a millimetre document shown in inches the Ø6 chip reads 0.236 in, a keypad 0.25 reads 0.25 in and stores 6.35 mm, and an M8 clearance hint reads 0.354 in. The first two browser cases fail without the fix. Remaining for M01: an untouched tapped through-hole's thread arrow reads the inactive blind depth (10 mm) while Done threads the whole bore (an M8 tapped through a 20 mm plate shows 10, cuts 20); the client has no exact through-bore length, so the arrow needs the built bore's length or no default reading before displayed value = committed geometry holds for it. QA15-2D-01 fix (schema v150, up from v148): a new hole's typed position is measured from the centroid of its entry face's outer boundary loop (`positionAnchor: 'outer-centroid'`, `FaceGeometry.outerCentroid`), so drilling or resizing another hole in the face no longer moves it; holes saved with `centroid` or no marker keep their anchor (`test/hole-outer-anchor.test.ts`: four 10 mm-inset holes on a 100 × 60 plate land where typed, resizing the first leaves the rest, and a document saved before v147 rebuilds its drifted holes unchanged). | S04/S05 for sketch-driven positions, U01 for handles; product F-3, design B2. Existing single-hole editor is shipped. |
| <a id="m02"></a>M02 | Partial | Feature/path/mirror patterns, per-instance suppression and pattern handles with correct overlap material and stable references; total-or-spacing definitions with quantity, uniform or rotated orientation for circular patterns, and one to three linear axes. Slice 1, feature patterns and per-instance suppression (schema v62, up from v61; no kernel change, Remus 2026.1.141). The `pattern` feature takes two optional fields. `sourceFeatureIds` repeats earlier holes and add/cut extrudes on the target body instead of copying the whole body. `skippedInstances` leaves copies out by their instance label (`"3"`, or `"1-2"` in a grid), so skipping one renames no other copy. A content gate refuses either field on a document that declares an older schema. Validation refuses: the original's label, a malformed or repeated label, an empty list, a later feature, a feature that is not a hole, boss or cut, and a to-face extrude. The build refuses by name a suppressed source, a source not made on the target's body chain, and a Move or Mirror between the source and the pattern. A skipped label that a lowered count no longer reaches is ignored with an advisory. The build (`packages/kernel-adapter/src/exact-feature-pattern.ts`) makes each feature's tool once at its original place: the hole's bore and counterbore through the shared `holeToolSolids`, or the extrude's swept profile. It repeats the tool through the same K05 arm as the whole-body pattern: the kernel's journaled linear and grid entry points and the closed ring where copies cannot overlap, rigid copies elsewhere. Copies are cut one tool at a time, or fused onto the body for a boss. Through extents reach across the target from wherever each copy lands. Each copy is proven: it must touch and change the body, a hole copy's bores must come back as analytic cylinders on its own axis, and the result must be one valid solid. A copy that misses refuses by its label, with "skip it or change the pattern". Faces the copies leave alone keep their names, and each hole copy's bore is named `pattern.face.instance.<label>.source.<hole feature id>.hole.face.bore`, so two repeated holes never share a name. Whole-body patterns drop skipped copies before the overlap fuse and keep the others' instance names. UI (U11 pick-and-drag, shared by Linear, Circular and Grid pattern): a Repeat option (Body, Features); in Features a click on a face picks the hole, boss or cut that made it. The spacing arrow and angle rig stand on the picked feature. Every copy wears a chip where it lands, and a click skips it or brings it back. A History edit reopens with the same features and skipped copies, and an untouched edit writes them back unchanged; a repeated feature whose faces the model no longer shows is kept as stored rather than dropped. The History row reads "× 3 · 1 skipped". Evidence: `test/feature-pattern.test.ts` (22 cases with closed-form volumes and bore positions: a through hole ×5 along X, two holes repeated with distinct copy names, a skipped copy without renaming, a 3 × 2 grid with one skipped, an edited hole the copies follow, a missing copy refused and then skipped, a through-all square cut around a ring, a boss row with one skipped, a moved body refused by name, whole-body skips with names kept, a stale skip advisory, a skipped copy of an overlapping full ring; plus document, command and v62-gate refusals); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts`, `edit.test.ts` and `featureSummary.test.ts`; `test/e2e/feature-pattern.spec.ts`, which picks a hole in place, picks its bore under Repeat Features, drags the spacing, clicks copy 1's chip to skip it, then reopens the pattern from History and clicks the chip to bring the copy back. Budget: the 10 × 10 grid preview (`node scripts/measure-active1-baselines.mjs --only pattern-node,pattern-browser`, 3 runs each) went from 7,540 ms before to 7,550 ms after in the browser, and from 7,684 ms to 7,631 ms in Node, against the 10,000 ms gate. Open: patterns driven by sketch points (next slice); path and mirror patterns; naming boss and cut copies' faces; repeating fillets, chamfers and other feature kinds; a total-or-spacing choice for linear patterns. Slice 2, patterns driven by sketch points (schema v63, up from v62; no kernel change, Remus 2026.1.141). A `pattern` with `patternKind: 'points'` stores `points`: an earlier sketch, the I01 point entities it stands on, and the reference point the original stands on. Each other point takes a copy moved there from the reference. A copy's instance label is its point's id, so adding, removing or moving another point renames no copy, and `skippedInstances` names points. It works for whole bodies and, with `sourceFeatureIds`, for holes, bosses and cuts, so slice 1's per-copy proofs and refusals apply unchanged. Every copy is a rigid copy through the K05 copy-and-fuse path: the kernel has no entry point for an arbitrary set of translations, and copies that overlap are fused as any pattern's are. Validation refuses: points on another kind, a missing sketch or point, an entity that is not a point, a later sketch, a reference that is not one of the points, only the reference, skipping the reference, and skipping a point the pattern does not stand on. The build refuses a point deleted since by name, and a moved point carries its copy. A content gate refuses a v63 pattern on an older document. UI (U11 pick-and-drag): a Point pattern tool in the Pattern group with the same Repeat option (Body, Features). After the body or the feature, clicks on the sketch's points (drawn as small crossed marks) choose where copies go. The tool, not the user, takes the reference: the sketch point nearest the original, and an edit keeps the one stored. Each copy wears the same skip chip where it lands. A History edit reopens with the points as picks and writes them back unchanged. The quick parameter preview leaves a pattern on points to the exact build, as slice 1 does for feature patterns and skipped copies. Evidence: `test/point-pattern.test.ts` (14 cases: a hole repeated at four points with closed-form volume, bore axes and per-point copy names (`pattern.face.instance.<point id>.source.<hole feature id>.hole.face.bore`); a skipped point; a moved point carrying its copy; whole-body copies apart and overlapping, fused to 2500 mm³; a deleted point refused by name; points added through an edit; and the document and v63-gate refusals); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts` and `edit.test.ts`; `test/e2e/point-pattern.spec.ts`, which drills a hole in place, picks its bore under Repeat Features, clicks two sketch points, skips one by its chip, and checks the stored pattern and its "1 skipped" History row. Budget: the 10 × 10 grid preview (`node scripts/measure-active1-baselines.mjs --only pattern-node,pattern-browser`, 3 runs each) measured 7,571 ms in the browser and 7,645 ms in Node on this branch, against the 10,000 ms gate (slice 1: 7,550 and 7,631 ms; main before M02: 7,540 and 7,684 ms). Open: path and mirror patterns; naming boss and cut copies' faces; repeating other feature kinds; a total-or-spacing choice for linear patterns. Named faces on boss and cut copies (no schema change). A feature pattern now names each repeated boss's or cut's copy faces after the faces of the feature it copies, `pattern.face.instance.<label>.source.<feature>.<its name>`, as slice 1 named hole copies' bores. A copy's face takes the name only where exactly one face of the result is the original's moved by the copy's placement: the same kind of surface on the moved carrier, with the moved centroid and the same perimeter, within a few quanta of the witnesses' rounding. A face the body truncates differently (a copy running off the plate's end, a boss merging into another) is not congruent and stays unnamed, as does a face two copies would both claim, so a name always means the copy's own face. The original keeps its names. Evidence: `test/pattern-copy-face-names.test.ts` (a boss row and a pocket row: each of the feature's own five faces on copies 1 and 2 named after the original's, each 25 further along; a pocket row whose last copy runs half off the plate leaves its floor and cut-short walls unnamed). Slice 3, patterns along a sketch path (schema v112, up from v111; no kernel change). A `pattern` with `patternKind: 'path'` stores `path`: an earlier sketch, the line and arc entities it runs along (construction ones included), and optionally `reversed: true` and `orientation: 'tangent'`. The curves are chained end to end, open or closed, in any listed order (`packages/geometry/src/path-stations.ts`): an open path starts at the free end of the earliest-listed of its two end curves, a closed one where its first-listed curve begins, and `reversed` starts at the other end. The original stands at the start; copy `i` stands `i × spacing` along the path by arc length (an arc's length is r·θ, so every station is closed form), moved there, and with `'tangent'` also turned in the sketch plane by how far the path has turned. Copies are labelled by their index as a linear pattern's are, so `skippedInstances` and copy names work unchanged, and it works for whole bodies and, with `sourceFeatureIds`, for holes, bosses and cuts with slice 1's per-copy proofs. Every copy is a rigid copy through the K05 copy-and-fuse path. Refused: a path on another kind, a missing sketch, a curve named twice, an entity that is not a line or arc, a later sketch, an unknown orientation, and below v112 by the content gate; at build, by name, curves that branch or do not join, a deleted curve, and copies that do not fit ("6 copies 20 apart need 100 of path, and the path is 80 long… Lower the count or the spacing", a closed path short of coming back onto the original). UI (U11 pick-and-drag): a Path pattern tool in the Pattern group with Repeat (Body, Features); after the body or the feature, clicks on sketch lines and arcs pick the path. The start is the end nearer the original (an edit keeps the stored one while the curves stay the same); Options hold Orientation (Moved, Follow path) and Start (Near end, Other end). One arrow along the path's first direction drags the spacing, each copy wears the skip chip, and a count that does not fit is named in the hint with the path's length. A History edit reopens with the curves as picks and writes the pattern back unchanged; History makes the sketch a parent of the pattern and an edit to the sketch revalidates it. Evidence: `test/path-pattern.test.ts` (16 cases: stations, turns and fit on a line and arc in closed form; a hole repeated four times along a line with bore axes, closed-form volume and per-copy names; copies carried round an arc from a reversed start; whole-body copies moved or turned with the path, by bounding box; copies past the end and a deleted curve refused by name; an edited count and spacing; the document and v112-gate refusals); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts`, `edit.test.ts`, `featureHistory.test.ts`, `affectedFeatureTargets.test.ts`; `test/e2e/path-pattern.spec.ts`, which drills a hole in place, picks its bore under Repeat Features, clicks the two lines of an L, drags the spacing arrow, skips copy 1 by its chip and checks the stored path and its "1 skipped" History row. The grid preview path the M02 budget measures is unchanged. Open: mirror patterns; naming boss and cut copies' faces; repeating fillets, chamfers and other feature kinds; a total-or-spacing choice for linear patterns. Slice 4, mirror patterns (schema v119, up from v117, which #1799 took; v118 is claimed by #1808; no kernel change). A `pattern` with `patternKind: 'mirror'` stores `mirrorPlane`, a datum plane or an origin and normal as Mirror takes it, and makes one copy: the whole body, or with `sourceFeatureIds` the repeated holes, bosses and cuts, reflected in the plane (x ↦ x − 2((x − p)·n)n). The copy is labelled "1", so slice 1's per-copy proofs, bore checks and names apply unchanged (a mirrored hole's bore is `pattern.face.instance.1.source.<hole>.hole.face.bore`); it is the rigid copy-and-transform build, fused with the original where they overlap. Refused: a plane on another kind, a mirror pattern without one, a plane that is neither a datum plane nor an origin and normal, a later or missing datum plane, below v119 by the content gate, and at build a zero normal and a skipped copy (no copy would be left). The plane's expressions are renamed with their parameters, a datum plane it reads counts as used and as its parent in History, and an edit to the datum revalidates it. UI (U11 pick-and-drag): a Mirror pattern tool in the Pattern group with Repeat (Body, Features). After the body or the feature, the plane is a principal or datum plane, or a flat face (under Features, one no repeated feature made), slid by the same offset arrow as Mirror's, or typed in Options. A History edit reopens with the plane as stored and writes it back unchanged; History reads "Mirrored". Evidence: `test/mirror-pattern.test.ts` (a hole mirrored across the plate with closed-form volume, bore axes and its copy's name; whole-body copies apart and overlapping, fused to 1600 mm³; a skipped copy refused; the document and v119-gate refusals); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts` and `edit.test.ts`; `test/e2e/mirror-pattern.spec.ts`, which drills a hole in place, picks its bore under Repeat Features, picks the plate's end face, drags the plane back over the plate and checks the stored plane and its "Mirrored" History row. Increasing an overlapping cylinder pattern from 11 to 12 and from 12 to 13 copies took 67.4 and 57.8 ms median to show the exact preview, 2.17× and 2.55× faster than the fixed historical build. Five spacing edits improved 1.09–1.19×. All nine tested configurations matched the historical mesh bytes, volume, topology counts and warnings. The [browser measurements](docs/performance/2026-11-20-overlapping-linear-pattern-builds.md) include the intersection-only comparison and raw samples. On a 25-copy row of overlapping cylinders, five new spacings showed an exact preview 2.11–2.54× faster than the historical release. A 13-copy row improved 1.81–1.995×. The [matched measurements](docs/performance/2026-11-20-parallel-linear-intersections.md) include the count-edit comparator, cold-count costs, raw samples and reproduction steps. Patterns of fillets and chamfers (no schema change: an older client refuses such a pattern by name rather than building it wrongly). A feature pattern's `sourceFeatureIds` may now name fillets and chamfers beside holes, bosses and cuts. Each copy takes the blend where it holds the edges the original rounded. The original's stored edge references are moved with the copy's placement, and each must find exactly one edge of the body there: the same kind of curve and length, with the moved ends and middle (or, for a closed edge, the moved centre and axis). The blend is then applied with the original's own size and options through the same entry point and acceptance rules as its build (`edgeModifierSettings`, shared with it). An approximate blend is disclosed as the original's is. Sources run in History order, so a pattern of a boss and its fillet rounds each boss copy. Refused by name: a blend stored before its edges were named ("pick its edges again"); a copy that finds no edge where the original's would be ("Copy 1 of Round finds no edge where Round's edge 1 would be; skip it, or repeat the feature it rounds first"); and the kernel's refusal of a copy, prefixed with the copy's label. In the workspace, Repeat Features takes a fillet or chamfer by a click on one of its faces, as it takes a hole, boss or cut. Evidence: `test/pattern-blends.test.ts` (on the pinned kernel, a 10 × 10 × 5 boss and the fillet on its four uprights repeated three times, each giving up (1 − π/4) × 5 per upright; the boss and a chamfer on its top edges repeated, each top millimetre a frustum from 10 × 10 to 8 × 8; the fillet repeated alone refused by its first copy). Patterns of ribs (no schema change: an older client refuses such a pattern by name rather than building it wrongly). A feature pattern's `sourceFeatureIds` may now name a rib. Each rib now names its faces as a wrap does: the body's names carried where the rib only trims a face, and each face it adds named after its own exact witness by a 128-bit name (`rib.face.w<name>`). A pattern makes the rib's material again against the body the rib was made on, as its build does (`buildRibSolid`), and adds each copy where it lands, one copy at a time, each proven as slice 1's are and to join the body (a copy whose fuse adds a separate piece is refused by its label); each copy's faces are named after the rib's, by copy. Refused by name: a rib that did not build ("Gusset did not build, so a pattern cannot repeat it; fix Gusset first"). In the workspace, Repeat Features takes a rib by a click on one of its faces. Evidence: `test/pattern-ribs.test.ts` (on the pinned kernel, an L bracket 20 deep: the rib's own faces named after it; its 2-thick gusset across the inside corner repeated three times 4 apart along Z, adding 3 × 625 to 7 500, each copy's faces named by copy; a rib whose profile closes off nothing refused by its pattern; a copy 20 along (1, 1, 0), clear of both legs, refused as not joining the bracket); `test/e2e/pattern-ribs.spec.ts` (`@ci-smoke`), which sets Repeat Features along Z, picks the gusset's side, drags the spacing arrow from 20 to between 2 and 4.5, and checks the stored pattern. Sharing the compiled kernel with exact intersection workers cut the median first count edit from 149.95 to 74.45 ms across twelve paired Chromium sessions. Six-session groups measured 152.85 → 74.30 ms and 149.35 → 75.10 ms. Each increased a linear pattern from 3 to 11 overlapping cylinders. [Measurements and reproduction](docs/performance/2026-11-20-pattern-worker-startup.md) Patterns of wraps (no schema change: an older client refuses such a pattern by name rather than building it wrongly). A feature pattern's `sourceFeatureIds` may now name a wrap, embossed or engraved, beside holes, bosses, cuts, fillets and chamfers. Each wrap now names its faces: the body's names are carried through where the wrap only trims a face (`carryAnalyticFaces`), and each face the wrap adds, on a surface the body had no face on, is named after its own exact witness by a 128-bit name of its witness and the exact places of its vertices (`wrap.face.w<name>`, `nameAddedFaces`; the 32-bit fingerprint could name two faces alike across an edit, and the witness alone a trim reflected across its middle). A cone is read by its exact surface. A face whose witness another shares, or whose surface cannot be read, stays unnamed, and an edit that reshapes a face renames it, so a reference never moves to another face. The wrap's build records the cylinder or cone it resolved; a pattern makes the wrap's material again from it and repeats it through the same arm as a boss (emboss) or cut (engrave), each copy proven as slice 1's are. An emboss's copies are fused one at a time, as the wrap fuses its own pieces: the kernel declines several wrapped copies in one pass though each fuses exactly alone. Each copy's faces are named after the wrap's, by copy. Refused by name: a wrap that did not build ("Badge did not build, so a pattern cannot repeat it; fix Badge first"). In the workspace, Repeat Features takes a wrap by a click on one of its faces. Evidence: `test/pattern-wraps.test.ts` (on the pinned kernel, a radius-10 post: the wrap's own faces named after it and the post's caps keeping theirs; a 6 × 4 badge embossed 1 deep repeated four times round the post at 4 × 24 × (11² − 10²)/20 added, reaching ±11 on both axes, with each copy's faces named by copy; a Ø4 disc engraved 1 deep repeated three times; a wrap that did not build refused by its pattern; across an edit that moves the badge, no name shared by two different faces; a badge on a frustum naming its raised cone; a triangle's raised face renamed when an edit reflects it); `test/e2e/pattern-wraps.spec.ts` (`@ci-smoke`), which picks the badge under Repeat Features, clicks the Z axis line above the post, sets Fixed step, drags the step rig, sets four copies 90° apart, and checks the stored pattern. Changing a grid’s second-axis count can reuse a byte-identical first row. A 10×3 to 10×10 edit on the 50-copy bar took 1572 to 918 ms median in three matched Chromium pairs. The [benchmark record](docs/performance/2026-11-20-grid-preview-row-reuse.md) includes the initial preview, count edits, and corrected historical timings. Complete orthogonal grids of overlapping rectangular stock reuse one exact row when a gap separates the rows. The browser medians for the 3×3, 10×3 and 10×10 Grid pattern previews on the ×50 bar fell from 655/2,324/6,977 ms to 573/871/1,427 ms. Three alternating pairs of full-render local builds used Chromium 156, Ryzen 9 5900XT and RTX 3090 with the same Remus 2026.1.151 pin. The 10×10 adapter produced 780 faces with zero warnings and zero replay on Done. The nested-pattern regression compares published topology, mesh, STEP and a downstream subtraction with the full fuse (`test/grid-row-fusion.test.ts`). See the [measurements](docs/performance/2026-11-20-grid-row-fusion.md). With a source-built Remus supporting strict candidate unification, exact spacing previews on 13 and 25 overlapping cylinders took 83.8 and 115.0 ms, respectively (2.19× and 2.64× versus the historical app). The first change to 11 copies remained slower. The [four-build browser measurements](docs/performance/2026-11-20-strict-pattern-unification.md) cover the kernel control, five spacings, matching geometry and reproduction. A total-or-spacing choice for linear patterns (schema v120, up from v119; no kernel change). A linear `pattern` may store `spacingMode: 'total'`: its `spacing` is then the distance from the original to the last copy, and the copies stand `spacing / (count − 1)` apart, so a changed count keeps the row's length. Absent keeps the spacing between copies, so every older pattern builds unchanged. Refused: the mode on any other kind, an unknown mode, and below v120 by the content gate; `clearData: ['spacingMode']` returns a pattern to the spacing between copies. The quick parameter preview shares the total out as the build does. UI (U11 pick-and-drag): the Linear pattern tool's Options gain Spacing (Between copies, Total length); with Total length the arrow is the last copy and drags the row's length, and each copy's chip stands at its share. A History edit reopens with Total and writes it back unchanged; switching back to Between copies clears the mode. History reads "× 4 over 60 mm". Evidence: `test/pattern-total-length.test.ts` (four blocks over 60 standing 20 apart; a hole row over 60 keeping its length from three to four copies, bore axes and volume in closed form; the document, clear and v120-gate refusals); `apps/web/src/lib/inPlace/tools.test.ts`, `edit.test.ts` and `featureSummary.test.ts`; `test/e2e/pattern-total-length.spec.ts`, which picks a block, chooses Total length, drags the last copy and checks the stored mode and its History row. The exact pattern builder shares one finished-solid face scan across its bore checks. On a 32-hole plate the median skip-click to matching rendered frame was 201.0 ms on the original build, 194.1 ms on the direct parent and 135.7 ms after reuse. The 12-hole and 32-hole comparison includes 108 edits. [Fixture and results](docs/performance/2026-11-20-point-pattern-bore-checks.md). Moving the last sketch point in a 100-hole pattern repeats three of 99 exact cuts. Median Apply to the matching changed frame fell from 1,293.40 ms originally and 838.05 ms on the direct parent to 411.55 ms across six browser sessions per build. Early and middle edits replay the cuts. [Fixture, timings and output checks](docs/performance/2026-11-20-point-pattern-cut-reuse.md). On the 32-hole point-pattern fixture, meshing the accepted solid alongside validation changed median skip-to-frame time from 134.3 to 109.9 ms (1.22× over the direct parent and 1.81× over the historical build). [Measurements, scope and reproduction](docs/performance/2026-11-21-point-pattern-display-mesh.md). Retaining a pattern copy button on skip or restore preserves keyboard focus and reduced median click-to-worker dispatch from 8.6 to 4.9 ms on a 32-hole plate. The matching exact frame arrived in 130.8 ms versus 136.0 ms on the parent. [Pattern copy controls, benchmark and keyboard reproduction](docs/performance/2026-11-21-pattern-copy-controls.md). Reusing a warm worker for the one new intersection in an 11-to-12 cylinder pattern edit reduced median keypad-to-exact-preview time from 71.55 to 68.40 ms. The original build took 137.55 ms. The two six-session groups reached 1.91× and 2.19× against that baseline.
